I’ll be standing there at bowling thinking about when I can leave, which is stupid because I used to enjoy it.\n\nDoctor: Did all of this start after a specific stressful event?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess there was. It’s not like one dramatic thing happened, but it kind of started around when the arguments at home got a lot worse.\n\nThat’s been… what, about a year and a couple months now? A year and two months, maybe. It’s funny, I can remember because it was around the time we were also dealing with this stupid dishwasher leak, and I kept thinking, “Okay, once this is fixed, things will calm down,” but then the fighting just sort of kept going. So… yeah, I’d say it started after that stuff at home really ramped up.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living, or thoughts of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: I… yeah, sort of. Not like I’m making a plan or anything like that. I don’t have some thing I’m about to do.\n\nIt’s more like… I’ll be lying there in the morning, or driving to work, and the thought just kind of pops in that maybe it’d be easier if I just didn’t wake up one day. Or that everyone would be better off not having to deal with me being like this.\n\nAnd then I feel guilty even thinking that, because, you know, people have real problems and I’m still going to work and all that… but yeah. It comes up more than I’d like to admit.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been feeling this down nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 37 / sales_manager / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Depressed_Mood\nmanifestation: \"Most days I feel like this heavy, gray mood follows me from the office to the car to the house, and even when nothing specific is happening I just feel kind of hollow.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

Yeah… most days, I think. It’s not like I wake up and say, “Today I’m going to be miserable,” it’s more like this heavy, gray feeling just sort of follows me around.

Yeah… I’m still functioning, I guess, if you look at it from the outside. I show up to work, I’m not just disappearing or anything. But it’s definitely… not the same.

It’s been wearing me down.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot about many different parts of your life, not just sleep?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “many different parts,” exactly. I mean, it kind of starts with one thing and then it spreads, if that makes sense. Like work will pop into my head, and then somehow I’m thinking about the house or some dumb thing I said to someone three years ago.\n\nBut I don’t know if that’s worrying, or just… my brain not shutting up. My wife would probably say I overthink things. She says that when we’re trying to watch TV and I’m checking my phone, but that’s a whole other thing. So yeah, I guess I’m not totally sure how to answer that.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t know if I would’ve called it “depressed” at first, because I’m still getting through the day and doing the normal stuff, mostly.\n\nBut most days it’s like this heavy, gray feeling just kind of follows me around — from the office to the car, then into the house. And even when nothing specific is going wrong, I feel sort of… hollow, I guess. Like I’m there, but not really.\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying things less than you usually do?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I have. That’s actually one of the weirder parts, because I used to have stuff I’d really look forward to. Like pickup basketball, bowling nights with a few guys, music festivals when we could make it work… and I’ve got this smoker I used to love firing up for friends.\n\nLately, though, I either cancel, or I go and I’m just kind of… there. Like I’m doing the thing I normally do, but I’m not really getting anything out of it. I’ll be standing there at bowling thinking about when I can leave, which is stupid because I used to enjoy it.\n\nDoctor: Did all of this start after a specific stressful event?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess there was. It’s not like one dramatic thing happened, but it kind of started around when the arguments at home got a lot worse.\n\nThat’s been… what, about a year and a couple months now? A year and two months, maybe. It’s funny, I can remember because it was around the time we were also dealing with this stupid dishwasher leak, and I kept thinking, “Okay, once this is fixed, things will calm down,” but then the fighting just sort of kept going. So… yeah, I’d say it started after that stuff at home really ramped up.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living, or thoughts of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: I… yeah, sort of. Not like I’m making a plan or anything like that. I don’t have some thing I’m about to do.\n\nIt’s more like… I’ll be lying there in the morning, or driving to work, and the thought just kind of pops in that maybe it’d be easier if I just didn’t wake up one day. Or that everyone would be better off not having to deal with me being like this.\n\nAnd then I feel guilty even thinking that, because, you know, people have real problems and I’m still going to work and all that… but yeah. It comes up more than I’d like to admit.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling this down nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most days, I think. It’s not like I wake up and say, “Today I’m going to be miserable,” it’s more like this heavy, gray feeling just sort of follows me around.\n\nLike I’ll leave the office, get in the car, come home, and it’s still there. And even if nothing is actually happening—like no argument, no big problem right in that moment—I still feel kind of hollow. So… yeah, it’s been pretty much most days.\n\nDoctor: Has this been making it hard for you to function at work or at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’m still functioning, I guess, if you look at it from the outside. I show up to work, I’m not just disappearing or anything. But it’s definitely… not the same.\n\nI manage a sales team, so there’s always forecasts and client prep and all these little moving pieces, and lately I’ll miss something or put it off until it’s too late. Then one of my team members has to jump in or smooth it over, which feels awful because I’m supposed to be the one keeping us on track. It’s like when my garage gets cluttered and I keep saying I’ll clean it Saturday, and then suddenly I can’t even find the screwdriver—sorry, that’s probably not the point.\n\nAt home, too… I’m there, but I’m not really there. My wife and kids will be talking and I’ll realize I haven’t been listening, or I’ll kind of check out instead of being involved like I used to. So yeah, it’s making things harder.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

I don’t know if I’d call it excessive. I mean, I worry about work, sure, and then normal family stuff, money things sometimes… but doesn’t everybody?

Most days… I’m not sure. Some days it’s more like background noise, and other days I’m too busy to even notice it, if that makes sense. Like yesterday I was thinking about a client presentation, but then my son needed help finding his cleats and the whole morning kind of disappeared. So I don’t know that I’m sitting there worrying all day.

I guess I’m having trouble judging what “excessive” means.

It comes up more than I’d like to admit.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling this down nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most days, I think. It’s not like I wake up and say, “Today I’m going to be miserable,” it’s more like this heavy, gray feeling just sort of follows me around.\n\nLike I’ll leave the office, get in the car, come home, and it’s still there. And even if nothing is actually happening—like no argument, no big problem right in that moment—I still feel kind of hollow. So… yeah, it’s been pretty much most days.\n\nDoctor: Has this been making it hard for you to function at work or at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’m still functioning, I guess, if you look at it from the outside. I show up to work, I’m not just disappearing or anything. But it’s definitely… not the same.\n\nI manage a sales team, so there’s always forecasts and client prep and all these little moving pieces, and lately I’ll miss something or put it off until it’s too late. Then one of my team members has to jump in or smooth it over, which feels awful because I’m supposed to be the one keeping us on track. It’s like when my garage gets cluttered and I keep saying I’ll clean it Saturday, and then suddenly I can’t even find the screwdriver—sorry, that’s probably not the point.\n\nAt home, too… I’m there, but I’m not really there. My wife and kids will be talking and I’ll realize I haven’t been listening, or I’ll kind of check out instead of being involved like I used to. So yeah, it’s making things harder.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your appetite changing lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s been kind of off. I don’t really sit down for lunch much anymore, which is weird because I used to be pretty regular about it. Now I’ll just have coffee and tell myself I’ll grab something after the next call, and then suddenly it’s like 3:30.\n\nDinner’s kind of the same. I’ll sit with my wife and the kids and sort of move food around or take a few bites. Although sometimes I wonder if that’s just because by then everyone’s talking over each other and I’m half distracted by the day—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nBut yeah, my appetite hasn’t been normal for a while. Months, probably. And I noticed my belt’s sitting a notch tighter than it used to, which doesn’t totally make sense to me, but it’s there.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period where you felt unusually up, energized, or irritable for several days in a row?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? Not in the way you mean, I guess.\n\nI mean, I have days where I’m keyed up because work is crazy—like if we’re trying to close out the quarter and everybody suddenly remembers they need pricing approved yesterday. Then I’m running around, answering emails, probably sounding short with people. But that feels more like stress than… being unusually up or energized.\n\nSeveral days in a row where I felt great or like I had all this extra energy? I can’t really think of a time like that. Irritable, maybe here and there, but not some big stretch that stands out.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your worrying feels hard to control?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I do worry, but it’s hard for me to tell what counts as “hard to control,” you know?\n\nSometimes I’ll start thinking about one thing, like work, and then it kind of turns into a whole chain of things, and suddenly I’m thinking about whether I forgot to schedule the car service or if the kids have something due for school—sorry, that’s probably wandering off.\n\nBut yeah, I don’t know. And even when nothing specific is going wrong, I feel sort of… hollow, I guess. Like I’m there, but not really.\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying things less than you usually do?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I have. That’s actually one of the weirder parts, because I used to have stuff I’d really look forward to. Like pickup basketball, bowling nights with a few guys, music festivals when we could make it work… and I’ve got this smoker I used to love firing up for friends.\n\nLately, though, I either cancel, or I go and I’m just kind of… there. Like I’m doing the thing I normally do, but I’m not really getting anything out of it. I’ll be standing there at bowling thinking about when I can leave, which is stupid because I used to enjoy it.\n\nDoctor: Did all of this start after a specific stressful event?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess there was. It’s not like one dramatic thing happened, but it kind of started around when the arguments at home got a lot worse.\n\nThat’s been… what, about a year and a couple months now? A year and two months, maybe. It’s funny, I can remember because it was around the time we were also dealing with this stupid dishwasher leak, and I kept thinking, “Okay, once this is fixed, things will calm down,” but then the fighting just sort of kept going. So… yeah, I’d say it started after that stuff at home really ramped up.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living, or thoughts of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: I… yeah, sort of. Not like I’m making a plan or anything like that. I don’t have some thing I’m about to do.\n\nIt’s more like… I’ll be lying there in the morning, or driving to work, and the thought just kind of pops in that maybe it’d be easier if I just didn’t wake up one day. Or that everyone would be better off not having to deal with me being like this.\n\nAnd then I feel guilty even thinking that, because, you know, people have real problems and I’m still going to work and all that… but yeah. It comes up more than I’d like to admit.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling this down nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most days, I think. It’s not like I wake up and say, “Today I’m going to be miserable,” it’s more like this heavy, gray feeling just sort of follows me around.\n\nLike I’ll leave the office, get in the car, come home, and it’s still there. And even if nothing is actually happening—like no argument, no big problem right in that moment—I still feel kind of hollow. So… yeah, it’s been pretty much most days.\n\nDoctor: Has this been making it hard for you to function at work or at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’m still functioning, I guess, if you look at it from the outside. I show up to work, I’m not just disappearing or anything. But it’s definitely… not the same.\n\nI manage a sales team, so there’s always forecasts and client prep and all these little moving pieces, and lately I’ll miss something or put it off until it’s too late. Then one of my team members has to jump in or smooth it over, which feels awful because I’m supposed to be the one keeping us on track. It’s like when my garage gets cluttered and I keep saying I’ll clean it Saturday, and then suddenly I can’t even find the screwdriver—sorry, that’s probably not the point.\n\nAt home, too… I’m there, but I’m not really there. My wife and kids will be talking and I’ll realize I haven’t been listening, or I’ll kind of check out instead of being involved like I used to. So yeah, it’s making things harder.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your appetite changing lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s been kind of off. I don’t really sit down for lunch much anymore, which is weird because I used to be pretty regular about it. Now I’ll just have coffee and tell myself I’ll grab something after the next call, and then suddenly it’s like 3:30.\n\nDinner’s kind of the same. I’ll sit with my wife and the kids and sort of move food around or take a few bites. Although sometimes I wonder if that’s just because by then everyone’s talking over each other and I’m half distracted by the day—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nBut yeah, my appetite hasn’t been normal for a while. Months, probably. And I noticed my belt’s sitting a notch tighter than it used to, which doesn’t totally make sense to me, but it’s there.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period where you felt unusually up, energized, or irritable for several days in a row?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? Not in the way you mean, I guess.\n\nI mean, I have days where I’m keyed up because work is crazy—like if we’re trying to close out the quarter and everybody suddenly remembers they need pricing approved yesterday. Then I’m running around, answering emails, probably sounding short with people. But that feels more like stress than… being unusually up or energized.\n\nSeveral days in a row where I felt great or like I had all this extra energy? I can’t really think of a time like that. Irritable, maybe here and there, but not some big stretch that stands out.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your worrying feels hard to control?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I do worry, but it’s hard for me to tell what counts as “hard to control,” you know?\n\nSometimes I’ll start thinking about one thing, like work, and then it kind of turns into a whole chain of things, and suddenly I’m thinking about whether I forgot to schedule the car service or if the kids have something due for school—sorry, that’s probably wandering off.\n\nBut yeah, I don’t know. I haven’t really paid attention to whether I can stop it or not. It’s just… there sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Would you say you’ve been worrying excessively about several different areas of your life on most days?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it excessive. I mean, I worry about work, sure, and then normal family stuff, money things sometimes… but doesn’t everybody?\n\nMost days… I’m not sure. Some days it’s more like background noise, and other days I’m too busy to even notice it, if that makes sense. Like yesterday I was thinking about a client presentation, but then my son needed help finding his cleats and the whole morning kind of disappeared. So I don’t know that I’m sitting there worrying all day.\n\nI guess I’m having trouble judging what “excessive” means.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually tired or low on energy nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. Tired, yeah, some days, but I don’t know if it’s “unusual” or nearly every day.\n\nI mean, I have two kids and work’s been… well, work is always kind of a lot. Sometimes I get home and I just sit in the car for a minute before going inside, but then again I feel like half the parents in my neighborhood probably do that. There’s this one dad who’s always out jogging at like 6 a.m., which makes me feel like I’m doing something wrong, but—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nLow energy… maybe? I haven’t really been tracking it. It’s hard to tell what’s normal tired and what’s something else.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Six months… I don’t know. I haven’t really been keeping track like that.

I mean, the worry comes and goes, I guess. There are stretches where I notice it more, especially if work is busy or there’s a lot happening at home. But trying to say “most days” for six months feels… I don’t know, hard to pin down. Six months ago was—what, spring? I remember we had that whole issue with the fence repair, and I was stressed about that, but that’s probably not what you mean.

So… I’m not sure. I can’t really say yes to that.

I start the day feeling like I didn’t really rest at all. So… yeah, I’d say it started after that stuff at home really ramped up.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living, or thoughts of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: I… yeah, sort of. Not like I’m making a plan or anything like that. I don’t have some thing I’m about to do.\n\nIt’s more like… I’ll be lying there in the morning, or driving to work, and the thought just kind of pops in that maybe it’d be easier if I just didn’t wake up one day. Or that everyone would be better off not having to deal with me being like this.\n\nAnd then I feel guilty even thinking that, because, you know, people have real problems and I’m still going to work and all that… but yeah. It comes up more than I’d like to admit.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling this down nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most days, I think. It’s not like I wake up and say, “Today I’m going to be miserable,” it’s more like this heavy, gray feeling just sort of follows me around.\n\nLike I’ll leave the office, get in the car, come home, and it’s still there. And even if nothing is actually happening—like no argument, no big problem right in that moment—I still feel kind of hollow. So… yeah, it’s been pretty much most days.\n\nDoctor: Has this been making it hard for you to function at work or at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’m still functioning, I guess, if you look at it from the outside. I show up to work, I’m not just disappearing or anything. Then one of my team members has to jump in or smooth it over, which feels awful because I’m supposed to be the one keeping us on track. It’s like when my garage gets cluttered and I keep saying I’ll clean it Saturday, and then suddenly I can’t even find the screwdriver—sorry, that’s probably not the point.\n\nAt home, too… I’m there, but I’m not really there. My wife and kids will be talking and I’ll realize I haven’t been listening, or I’ll kind of check out instead of being involved like I used to. So yeah, it’s making things harder.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your appetite changing lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s been kind of off. I don’t really sit down for lunch much anymore, which is weird because I used to be pretty regular about it. I’ll sit with my wife and the kids and sort of move food around or take a few bites. Not in the way you mean, I guess.\n\nI mean, I have days where I’m keyed up because work is crazy—like if we’re trying to close out the quarter and everybody suddenly remembers they need pricing approved yesterday. Then I’m running around, answering emails, probably sounding short with people. But that feels more like stress than… being unusually up or energized.\n\nSeveral days in a row where I felt great or like I had all this extra energy? I can’t really think of a time like that. Irritable, maybe here and there, but not some big stretch that stands out.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your worrying feels hard to control?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I haven’t really paid attention to whether I can stop it or not. It’s just… there sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Would you say you’ve been worrying excessively about several different areas of your life on most days?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it excessive. I don’t have some thing I’m about to do.\n\nIt’s more like… I’ll be lying there in the morning, or driving to work, and the thought just kind of pops in that maybe it’d be easier if I just didn’t wake up one day. Or that everyone would be better off not having to deal with me being like this.\n\nAnd then I feel guilty even thinking that, because, you know, people have real problems and I’m still going to work and all that… but yeah. It comes up more than I’d like to admit.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling this down nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most days, I think. It’s not like I wake up and say, “Today I’m going to be miserable,” it’s more like this heavy, gray feeling just sort of follows me around.\n\nLike I’ll leave the office, get in the car, come home, and it’s still there. And even if nothing is actually happening—like no argument, no big problem right in that moment—I still feel kind of hollow. So… yeah, it’s been pretty much most days.\n\nDoctor: Has this been making it hard for you to function at work or at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’m still functioning, I guess, if you look at it from the outside. I show up to work, I’m not just disappearing or anything. But it’s definitely… not the same.\n\nI manage a sales team, so there’s always forecasts and client prep and all these little moving pieces, and lately I’ll miss something or put it off until it’s too late. Then one of my team members has to jump in or smooth it over, which feels awful because I’m supposed to be the one keeping us on track. It’s like when my garage gets cluttered and I keep saying I’ll clean it Saturday, and then suddenly I can’t even find the screwdriver—sorry, that’s probably not the point.\n\nAt home, too… I’m there, but I’m not really there. My wife and kids will be talking and I’ll realize I haven’t been listening, or I’ll kind of check out instead of being involved like I used to. So yeah, it’s making things harder.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your appetite changing lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s been kind of off. I don’t really sit down for lunch much anymore, which is weird because I used to be pretty regular about it. Now I’ll just have coffee and tell myself I’ll grab something after the next call, and then suddenly it’s like 3:30.\n\nDinner’s kind of the same. I’ll sit with my wife and the kids and sort of move food around or take a few bites. Although sometimes I wonder if that’s just because by then everyone’s talking over each other and I’m half distracted by the day—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nBut yeah, my appetite hasn’t been normal for a while. Months, probably. And I noticed my belt’s sitting a notch tighter than it used to, which doesn’t totally make sense to me, but it’s there.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period where you felt unusually up, energized, or irritable for several days in a row?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? Not in the way you mean, I guess.\n\nI mean, I have days where I’m keyed up because work is crazy—like if we’re trying to close out the quarter and everybody suddenly remembers they need pricing approved yesterday. Then I’m running around, answering emails, probably sounding short with people. But that feels more like stress than… being unusually up or energized.\n\nSeveral days in a row where I felt great or like I had all this extra energy? I can’t really think of a time like that. Irritable, maybe here and there, but not some big stretch that stands out.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your worrying feels hard to control?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I do worry, but it’s hard for me to tell what counts as “hard to control,” you know?\n\nSometimes I’ll start thinking about one thing, like work, and then it kind of turns into a whole chain of things, and suddenly I’m thinking about whether I forgot to schedule the car service or if the kids have something due for school—sorry, that’s probably wandering off.\n\nBut yeah, I don’t know. I haven’t really paid attention to whether I can stop it or not. It’s just… there sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Would you say you’ve been worrying excessively about several different areas of your life on most days?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it excessive. I mean, I worry about work, sure, and then normal family stuff, money things sometimes… but doesn’t everybody?\n\nMost days… I’m not sure. Some days it’s more like background noise, and other days I’m too busy to even notice it, if that makes sense. Sometimes I get home and I just sit in the car for a minute before going inside, but then again I feel like half the parents in my neighborhood probably do that. There’s this one dad who’s always out jogging at like 6 a.m., which makes me feel like I’m doing something wrong, but—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nLow energy… maybe? I haven’t really been tracking it. It’s hard to tell what’s normal tired and what’s something else.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling this kind of worry on most days for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Six months… I don’t know. I remember we had that whole issue with the fence repair, and I was stressed about that, but that’s probably not what you mean.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I can’t really say yes to that.\n\nDoctor: When you can’t sleep, is your mind usually quiet, or is it filled with thoughts?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely not quiet. I wish it were. I’ll lie there and it’s like my brain starts running through work stuff—emails I forgot, conversations with clients, things I should’ve said differently—and then somehow it turns into replaying arguments at home too.\n\nAnd then I wake up at like 3 or 4 and it starts all over again. That’s actually kind of what pushed me to call the employee assistance program, because I’m getting up feeling like I didn’t really sleep at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your mood has been mostly low only because of the problems at home?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s only the home stuff. My wife would probably say I overthink things. She says that when we’re trying to watch TV and I’m checking my phone, but that’s a whole other thing. So yeah, I guess I’m not totally sure how to answer that.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t know if I would’ve called it “depressed” at first, because I’m still getting through the day and doing the normal stuff, mostly.\n\nBut most days it’s like this heavy, gray feeling just kind of follows me around — from the office to the car, then into the house. And even when nothing specific is going wrong, I feel sort of… hollow, I guess. Like I’m there, but not really.\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying things less than you usually do?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I have. That’s actually one of the weirder parts, because I used to have stuff I’d really look forward to. Like pickup basketball, bowling nights with a few guys, music festivals when we could make it work… and I’ve got this smoker I used to love firing up for friends.\n\nLately, though, I either cancel, or I go and I’m just kind of… there. Like I’m doing the thing I normally do, but I’m not really getting anything out of it. I’ll be standing there at bowling thinking about when I can leave, which is stupid because I used to enjoy it.\n\nDoctor: Did all of this start after a specific stressful event?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess there was. It’s not like one dramatic thing happened, but it kind of started around when the arguments at home got a lot worse.\n\nThat’s been… what, about a year and a couple months now? A year and two months, maybe. It’s funny, I can remember because it was around the time we were also dealing with this stupid dishwasher leak, and I kept thinking, “Okay, once this is fixed, things will calm down,” but then the fighting just sort of kept going. So… yeah, I’d say it started after that stuff at home really ramped up.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living, or thoughts of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: I… yeah, sort of. Not like I’m making a plan or anything like that. I don’t have some thing I’m about to do.\n\nIt’s more like… I’ll be lying there in the morning, or driving to work, and the thought just kind of pops in that maybe it’d be easier if I just didn’t wake up one day. Or that everyone would be better off not having to deal with me being like this.\n\nAnd then I feel guilty even thinking that, because, you know, people have real problems and I’m still going to work and all that… but yeah. It comes up more than I’d like to admit.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling this down nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most days, I think. It’s not like I wake up and say, “Today I’m going to be miserable,” it’s more like this heavy, gray feeling just sort of follows me around.\n\nLike I’ll leave the office, get in the car, come home, and it’s still there. And even if nothing is actually happening—like no argument, no big problem right in that moment—I still feel kind of hollow. So… yeah, it’s been pretty much most days.\n\nDoctor: Has this been making it hard for you to function at work or at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’m still functioning, I guess, if you look at it from the outside. I show up to work, I’m not just disappearing or anything. But it’s definitely… not the same.\n\nI manage a sales team, so there’s always forecasts and client prep and all these little moving pieces, and lately I’ll miss something or put it off until it’s too late. Then one of my team members has to jump in or smooth it over, which feels awful because I’m supposed to be the one keeping us on track. It’s like when my garage gets cluttered and I keep saying I’ll clean it Saturday, and then suddenly I can’t even find the screwdriver—sorry, that’s probably not the point.\n\nAt home, too… I’m there, but I’m not really there. My wife and kids will be talking and I’ll realize I haven’t been listening, or I’ll kind of check out instead of being involved like I used to. So yeah, it’s making things harder.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your appetite changing lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s been kind of off. I don’t really sit down for lunch much anymore, which is weird because I used to be pretty regular about it. Now I’ll just have coffee and tell myself I’ll grab something after the next call, and then suddenly it’s like 3:30.\n\nDinner’s kind of the same. I’ll sit with my wife and the kids and sort of move food around or take a few bites. Although sometimes I wonder if that’s just because by then everyone’s talking over each other and I’m half distracted by the day—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nBut yeah, my appetite hasn’t been normal for a while. Months, probably. And I noticed my belt’s sitting a notch tighter than it used to, which doesn’t totally make sense to me, but it’s there.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period where you felt unusually up, energized, or irritable for several days in a row?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? Not in the way you mean, I guess.\n\nI mean, I have days where I’m keyed up because work is crazy—like if we’re trying to close out the quarter and everybody suddenly remembers they need pricing approved yesterday. Then I’m running around, answering emails, probably sounding short with people. But that feels more like stress than… being unusually up or energized.\n\nSeveral days in a row where I felt great or like I had all this extra energy? I can’t really think of a time like that. Irritable, maybe here and there, but not some big stretch that stands out.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your worrying feels hard to control?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I do worry, but it’s hard for me to tell what counts as “hard to control,” you know?\n\nSometimes I’ll start thinking about one thing, like work, and then it kind of turns into a whole chain of things, and suddenly I’m thinking about whether I forgot to schedule the car service or if the kids have something due for school—sorry, that’s probably wandering off.\n\nBut yeah, I don’t know. I haven’t really paid attention to whether I can stop it or not. It’s just… there sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Would you say you’ve been worrying excessively about several different areas of your life on most days?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it excessive. I mean, I worry about work, sure, and then normal family stuff, money things sometimes… but doesn’t everybody?\n\nMost days… I’m not sure. Some days it’s more like background noise, and other days I’m too busy to even notice it, if that makes sense. Like yesterday I was thinking about a client presentation, but then my son needed help finding his cleats and the whole morning kind of disappeared. So I don’t know that I’m sitting there worrying all day.\n\nI guess I’m having trouble judging what “excessive” means.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually tired or low on energy nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. Tired, yeah, some days, but I don’t know if it’s “unusual” or nearly every day.\n\nI mean, I have two kids and work’s been… well, work is always kind of a lot. Sometimes I get home and I just sit in the car for a minute before going inside, but then again I feel like half the parents in my neighborhood probably do that. There’s this one dad who’s always out jogging at like 6 a.m., which makes me feel like I’m doing something wrong, but—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nLow energy… maybe? I haven’t really been tracking it. It’s hard to tell what’s normal tired and what’s something else.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling this kind of worry on most days for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Six months… I don’t know. I haven’t really been keeping track like that.\n\nI mean, the worry comes and goes, I guess. There are stretches where I notice it more, especially if work is busy or there’s a lot happening at home. But trying to say “most days” for six months feels… I don’t know, hard to pin down. Six months ago was—what, spring? I remember we had that whole issue with the fence repair, and I was stressed about that, but that’s probably not what you mean.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I can’t really say yes to that.\n\nDoctor: When you can’t sleep, is your mind usually quiet, or is it filled with thoughts?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely not quiet. I wish it were. I’ll lie there and it’s like my brain starts running through work stuff—emails I forgot, conversations with clients, things I should’ve said differently—and then somehow it turns into replaying arguments at home too.\n\nAnd then I wake up at like 3 or 4 and it starts all over again. That’s actually kind of what pushed me to call the employee assistance program, because I’m getting up feeling like I didn’t really sleep at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your mood has been mostly low only because of the problems at home?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s only the home stuff. I mean, home doesn’t help, obviously, and I can get stuck thinking about things there… but it’s not like I’m fine until I walk in the door.\n\nMost days it feels like this heavy, gray mood just kind of follows me around—from the office to the car to the house. Even if nothing specific is happening, I’ll just feel sort of… hollow, I guess. Like there isn’t one clear problem I can point to and say, “That’s why.”\n\nDoctor: Have you had trouble concentrating lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. It’s been kind of frustrating, honestly.\n\nLike, I used to be able to knock out sales forecasts or put together a client deck pretty quickly, and now I’ll be sitting there staring at the same slide, reading it five times, and I still don’t really absorb it. Then I start second-guessing every number, like, “Is that right? Should I phrase it differently?” and somehow I can’t decide what to actually say.\n\nWhich is ridiculous because this is stuff I’ve done for years. I mean, the templates are basically the same, half the time. But lately it just takes me forever to get through them.\n\nDoctor: Did your sleep problems start before your mood became low?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure which came first, honestly. The sleep thing is what finally made me call the employee assistance program, though, because I’m barely sleeping right.\n\nI’ll lie there replaying work stuff, and then arguments at home, and it just loops. Then I wake up around 3 or 4 and it’s like my brain picks up right where it left off. By the time I get up, I feel like I never really rested at all.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if “worthless” is the word I’d normally use, but… I do get stuck in that place.

I’ll start replaying stuff—every late night I stayed at work, or, like, going to tailgates and spending money on dumb things when I probably should’ve been home or saving it. So… yeah, I’d say it started after that stuff at home really ramped up.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living, or thoughts of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: I… yeah, sort of. Or that everyone would be better off not having to deal with me being like this.\n\nAnd then I feel guilty even thinking that, because, you know, people have real problems and I’m still going to work and all that… but yeah. And even if nothing is actually happening—like no argument, no big problem right in that moment—I still feel kind of hollow. So… yeah, it’s been pretty much most days.\n\nDoctor: Has this been making it hard for you to function at work or at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’m still functioning, I guess, if you look at it from the outside. But it’s definitely… not the same.\n\nI manage a sales team, so there’s always forecasts and client prep and all these little moving pieces, and lately I’ll miss something or put it off until it’s too late. Then one of my team members has to jump in or smooth it over, which feels awful because I’m supposed to be the one keeping us on track. It’s like when my garage gets cluttered and I keep saying I’ll clean it Saturday, and then suddenly I can’t even find the screwdriver—sorry, that’s probably not the point.\n\nAt home, too… I’m there, but I’m not really there. My wife and kids will be talking and I’ll realize I haven’t been listening, or I’ll kind of check out instead of being involved like I used to. So yeah, it’s making things harder.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your appetite changing lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s been kind of off. I don’t really sit down for lunch much anymore, which is weird because I used to be pretty regular about it. Now I’ll just have coffee and tell myself I’ll grab something after the next call, and then suddenly it’s like 3:30.\n\nDinner’s kind of the same. I’ll sit with my wife and the kids and sort of move food around or take a few bites. Not in the way you mean, I guess.\n\nI mean, I have days where I’m keyed up because work is crazy—like if we’re trying to close out the quarter and everybody suddenly remembers they need pricing approved yesterday. Then I’m running around, answering emails, probably sounding short with people. But that feels more like stress than… being unusually up or energized.\n\nSeveral days in a row where I felt great or like I had all this extra energy? I can’t really think of a time like that. Irritable, maybe here and there, but not some big stretch that stands out.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your worrying feels hard to control?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I do worry, but it’s hard for me to tell what counts as “hard to control,” you know?\n\nSometimes I’ll start thinking about one thing, like work, and then it kind of turns into a whole chain of things, and suddenly I’m thinking about whether I forgot to schedule the car service or if the kids have something due for school—sorry, that’s probably wandering off.\n\nBut yeah, I don’t know. I haven’t really paid attention to whether I can stop it or not. I haven’t really been tracking it. It’s hard to tell what’s normal tired and what’s something else.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling this kind of worry on most days for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Six months… I don’t know.
